Put a person in a field on air: park, raise the mast or acquire the satellite, establish audio return and talkback, and conduct an interview across a path with a quarter-second to three seconds of delay each way.

- **Who.** Reporter, Camera Operator, Satellite Truck Engineer / Bonded-Cellular Operator
- **Decides.** Transmission method; go live or record and play; how long a pause the presenter leaves after each question; whether the reporter gets full talkback or programme only; whether to do it at all with the signal you have.
- **Engine.** Latency must be per-path and must apply to the conversation itself: at two seconds of bonded-cellular delay, presenter and reporter talk over each other unless the presenter deliberately waits. Path quality varies with location, weather and network load.
- **Takes.** 45 minutes to establish a satellite position; about 5 minutes with bonded cellular; live hits every 30-60 minutes thereafter

#### Audience management

Fill the seats, brief the room, warm it up, teach it when to applaud and when to be silent, hold it through every break, and keep it there for the length of a four-hour show without letting the energy die.

- **Who.** Audience Wrangler, Warm-up Artist, Floor Manager
- **Decides.** Audience size and composition; the warm-up approach; whether applause is cued or free; whether to seed the room with claques; when to release them.
- **Engine.** The audience is simultaneously an audio source and a reaction source, with energy that decays over time and spikes with events. A flat room measurably damages a comedy or entertainment show; an over-hot room steps on dialogue and breaks the mix.
- **Takes.** Doors 60-90 minutes before; warm-up 20-30 minutes; managed continuously throughout

#### Officiating and review integration

Serve the officials and the audience at the same time: feed angles to the review official, cover the review without pre-judging it, carry the review graphics, and resume the instant the decision comes.

- **Who.** Match Director, Replay Official Liaison, VAR / Hawk-Eye Operator
- **Decides.** Which angles to offer the official; whether to show the review live; whether to show a frame that contradicts the decision; what to fill the stoppage with.
- **Engine.** A review sub-state that halts the event for an unpredictable 20 seconds to 4 minutes with its own graphics and audio treatment. The broadcast must fill that time and be ready to resume instantly, mid-sentence, when play restarts.
- **Takes.** Unpredictable; several per event in codes that use it

#### Live captioning and access services

Produce captions for live output at 150-250 words per minute with two to six seconds of lag, by stenography or by respeaking into trained speech recognition; supply audio description and in-vision signing where required.

- **Who.** Stenocaptioner / Respeaker, Access Services Coordinator
- **Decides.** Method — steno, respeak or hybrid; whether to pre-load the scripted sections; the correction policy when a name is wrong; where the signer sits in frame and on which output.
- **Engine.** Captions as a delayed text stream with an accuracy rate that degrades with speech rate, accent, overlapping speakers and unfamiliar proper nouns. Regulators impose caption quotas and quality expectations that persist as licence obligations.
- **Takes.** Live throughout; pre-loading the scripted portions takes about an hour

#### Automated production control

Run the programme from the rundown itself: each row carries camera, switcher, graphics, audio and server commands as a macro, and one operator fires the sequence instead of five people executing independently on verbal cues.

- **Who.** Automation Operator / Technical Director (Ross OverDrive, Sony ELC, Vizrt Mosart, Grass Valley Ignite)
- **Decides.** Whether to automate the format; how much manual override to keep live; how to size the gallery; what happens to a fired macro when the rundown row is changed thirty seconds before it plays.
- **Engine.** Automation compiles a rundown row into a device-command macro. It removes human error and adds brittleness: a late rundown edit fires a stale macro. Crew cost falls and recovery capability falls with it — the engine must price both sides.
- **Takes.** Templates built over weeks; in practice saves roughly four to six gallery positions per show (estimate)

#### Live drama: transitions and the quick change

On a live drama or live musical the scenes are continuous: the cast walk from set to set while cameras and booms follow, scenery moves under them, and a full costume change happens in the forty seconds of an ad break.

- **Who.** Director, Floor Manager, Technical Manager, Costume Supervisor
- **Decides.** Set adjacency and running order; which transitions are covered by a wide, a VT, a musical number or a break; how long each change needs; what is pre-recorded as insurance against the transition that cannot be made.
- **Engine.** A floor plan with sets, cable paths and travel times. Actor, camera, boom and costume each have a position and a speed; every transition has a duration that must fit inside the item covering it. A transition that does not fit is a visible on-air failure.
- **Takes.** 3-6 weeks of rehearsal; transitions drilled dozens of times; performed exactly once

#### Transmission

The show happens once, in order, starting at a fixed second and ending at a fixed second. Every decision is final at the moment it is taken. What is recorded afterwards is the record of what you did, not a draft of it.

- **Who.** The gallery, all of it, at once
- **Decides.** Nothing new — this is the execution of everything already decided, plus every recovery from everything that fails while you are executing it.
- **Engine.** A single irreversible timeline with no undo anywhere in the stack. The engine must structurally forbid rollback: no rewind, no retake, no correction except a subsequent on-air action — an apology, a correction caption, a re-run of the item.
- **Takes.** 22 minutes to 5 hours; Olympic-scale coverage runs 12-18 hours a day for 17 days

#### As-run, archive and compliance recording

After air: the as-run log of what actually played against what was scheduled, the compliance recording the licence requires you to retain, the isos archived, and the clips cut for news, social and highlights.

- **Who.** Media Manager, Archive Operator, Transmission Controller
- **Decides.** What is retained and at what quality; how deeply the as-run is logged; what goes to the clips desk; what is restricted; when the highlights embargo lifts.
- **Engine.** Post-transmission is strictly DERIVATIVE — it copies a finished thing. Retention obligations (Ofcom requires UK television recordings to be kept for 60 days), clip embargoes and archive value are modelled separately from the live show itself.
- **Takes.** As-run is automatic; highlights cut within 5-30 minutes; archive ingest over days

#### De-rig and turnaround

Strike everything, coil every cable, load the tenders, and drive to the next venue — often overnight — in order to rig it again tomorrow morning for another live show.

- **Who.** Riggers, Rigger-Drivers, Engineer in Charge
- **Decides.** De-rig order; what travels with the truck and what goes ahead on a tender; driver hours and rest; whether to buy a second crew for a double weekend.
- **Engine.** Crew and drivers have legally binding hours and rest requirements. A Saturday-Sunday double is only possible if the de-rig fits inside the drive plus the rest. Fatigue must raise error probability on the following show.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours after a match; 1-3 days after a stadium show

#### Debrief and fault log

Log every fault, every near miss, every frame of black, every wrong super and every editorial complaint, and decide what changes before the next one. This is the only feedback loop live television has.

- **Who.** Engineer in Charge, Executive Producer, Technical Manager
- **Decides.** What gets fixed; what gets re-rehearsed; who does not get booked again; what kit is replaced; what becomes standing procedure across the whole operation.
- **Engine.** A persistent incident record feeding forward into future hazard rates, crew reputations and facility reliability. This is the campaign-level memory that replaces 'we'll fix it in post' — the only place improvement can accumulate.
- **Takes.** 30 minutes immediately after the show; a formal review within days of a major event

---

## 8. Commercials and branded content (advertising film: TV/cinema spots, digital and social films, brand content, product/tabletop work)

*made for a client, through an agency, against a pre-approved board, at a per-day cost nothing else in film comes near.*

### The unit of work

THE UNIT IS THE SETUP AND THE APPROVAL, NEVER THE PAGE. A commercial has no pages — the "script" is a board of 12-30 numbered frames, and a 30-second film contains roughly 15-25 cuts. The 1st AD builds the day as an ordered setup list with clock estimates (light / rehearse / turn over / reset), not as eighths. A live-action feature day = 4-5 pages. A commercial day = 20-40 setups, or, on a tabletop/food day, 3-6 setups shot 60 times each. Finished-film yield per shoot day is 10-30 seconds; a stop-motion analogy is fair — you are measuring output in seconds, not minutes. A GOOD DAY: every boarded setup in the can, plus the two "options" the agency asked for and the safety the client asked for; the product beauty pass covered before the food/pack dies or the light goes; the agency's preferred take circled at the monitor with the client physically nodding; a wrap inside the 10th or 12th hour with no meal penalty; and nothing added to the post fix-list. A day where you shot brilliantly but missed the pack shot is a failed day, because the pack shot is the only compulsory frame. PROGRESS ACROSS THE JOB IS COUNTED IN APPROVALS CROSSED, NOT FOOTAGE: brief accepted → territory chosen → client buys the board → legal substantiates the claim → budget released → bid awarded → PPM signed off → shoot approved live at the monitor → offline approved up the ladder → online/grade/mix approved → clearance body issues a clock number/Ad-ID → versions QC'd → trafficked before the deadline. Each gate has a named human with a veto. Time between gates is the real schedule. SCHEDULE SHAPE AND DURATION (typical national TV campaign, est.): - Brief to client concept approval: 2-6 weeks. - Bid out to three companies, treatments back, award: 8-15 working days (treatment 3-7 days, bid build 3-7 days, levelling 2-5 days). - Award to shoot ("prep"): 10-20 working days. That is the entire prep: crew, cast, wardrobe, locations, build, product, permits, PPM. A feature's 8-12 weeks compressed into two. - PPM: one 3-6 hour meeting, 2-4 days before the shoot. - Shoot: 1-3 days. Two days is normal. A day is 10 or 12 hours plus pre-call. - Offline: 1-3 weeks, 3-8 client rounds. - Online, VFX, grade, sound, versions: 1-3 weeks. - Clearance: 2-10 working days, in parallel and again at final. - Traffic deadline: 2-5 business days before first air. - Brief to air: 8-16 weeks typical; 6 weeks fast; 6 months for a global platform launch. - Reactive social/branded content: whole cycle in 3-10 days, shot on a half-day, cut overnight. - Actualization/final cost report: 30-90 days AFTER delivery. Talent usage administration: continues for 13-week cycles across a maximum 21-month period of use under the SAG-AFTRA Commercials Contract, then renegotiate or take it off air.

### What is absent

- DEVELOPMENT AND FINANCING ENTIRELY. No optioned IP, no spec scripts, no slate, no greenlight committee, no financiers, no equity/gap/pre-sales, no sales agent, no completion bond, no tax-credit-backed financing structure. The client wires money against a purchase order. There is no risk capital and no upside — the production company earns a fee, never a percentage.
- THE SCREENPLAY AND EVERYTHING BUILT ON IT. No 90-120 page script, no drafts, no writers' room, no rewrite cycle, no page count, no eighths, no stripboard, no day-out-of-days derived from scene/character intersections, no script breakdown in the AD sense. The board (12-30 frames) replaces it and is approved by the client before any director is hired.
- THE DIRECTOR'S AUTHORSHIP. No final cut, no director's cut clause, no auteur credit above title. The agency owns the edit; the director is a vendor executing a pre-sold idea. A director who 'improves' the board without permission has damaged the job, not elevated it.
- DISTRIBUTION AND EXHIBITION. No release date strategy, no windows, no territories sold, no festivals, no theatrical P&A, no box office, no streaming licence, no back end, no profit participation, no residual on the WORK (residuals exist only as talent usage). No unit publicist, no EPK, no junket, no poster campaign — the film IS the campaign.
- RATINGS AND CERTIFICATION. No MPA/CARA rating, no BBFC certificate. Replaced by a structurally different thing: pre-broadcast clearance of CLAIMS and content by a clearance body (Clearcast in the UK, network clearance departments in the US) with substantiation evidence attached.
- TEST SCREENINGS AND RECRUITED AUDIENCES. Replaced by copy testing/pre-testing of an animatic against category norms BEFORE the money is spent, and sometimes again at rough cut.
- THE LONG POST TAIL. No 20-week editorial, no locked-picture-to-mix runway, no spotting session with a composer, no scoring stage, no music editor, no dialogue editor, no ADR stage days, no Foley walker, no supervising sound editor, no predubs, no 5.1 near-field-to-large-room progression on most jobs. One sound designer and a mixer in one room, in one to two days.
- SECOND UNIT IN THE FEATURE SENSE, and the standing second-unit director. A tabletop/product unit exists but is a different discipline, not a coverage overflow.
- REHEARSAL PERIODS, table reads, character work, coach-led prep. Talent is typically met at callback and directed on the day, for one day.
- THE STUDIO/NETWORK EXECUTIVE LAYER, production executives, notes from a development VP. Replaced by a wholly different authority chain: agency creatives, agency account team, brand manager, marketing director, CMO, client legal.
- POST SUPERVISOR as a dedicated hire on most jobs — the agency producer supervises post directly, and the post houses are separately contracted vendors.
- SEQUELS, SERIES BIBLES, CONTINUITY ACROSS INSTALMENTS as a production concern, and dailies screenings for absent decision-makers — the decision-makers were standing behind the monitor.

### What is reordered

- THE BUDGET COMES BEFORE THE JOB AND IS THE PITCH. In features you develop, then schedule, then budget. Here three production companies build a full line-item AICP budget and a complete creative interpretation for a job none of them has, simultaneously, and two of them do it for nothing. The bid IS the audition.
- THE SCRIPT IS LOCKED AND CLIENT-APPROVED BEFORE THE DIRECTOR EXISTS. The board is written, internally killed and sharpened, legally substantiated, sometimes consumer-tested, and bought by the client — all before a director sees it. The director is hired to interpret a fixed artefact, in reverse of every other form.
- LEGAL AND CLEARANCE MOVE TO THE FRONT AND THEN HAPPEN AGAIN AT THE END. Claim substantiation gates the concept (a demo must be reproducible as performed, an unsupportable claim kills the idea before a bid). Then final clearance gates the finished film, after all the money is spent. E&O-at-delivery is replaced by pass/fail regulatory approval bracketing the whole job.
- CASTING HAPPENS AFTER THE JOB IS AWARDED, INSIDE PREP, AND THE CLIENT PICKS. In a two-week prep, casting runs in parallel with locations, wardrobe and build. The face is chosen by a brand manager from a selects tape, frequently over the director's preference, and that choice is final.
- USAGE IS NEGOTIATED BEFORE CASTING FINISHES AND DRIVES IT. The term/media/territory decision (13-week cycles vs 1-year vs 3-year worldwide buyout; union vs non-union) sets what a performer costs across two years, so the rights deal reshapes who you can afford to see.
- MUSIC IS CHOSEN BEFORE THE PICTURE. Track search starts at treatment stage; the board is often boarded to a track; the edit is cut to the licensed music rather than scored to the cut. Sound can be locked before picture — the inverse of a feature.
- THE EDITOR IS ATTACHED BEFORE THE SHOOT, AT AN INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL COMPANY, hired by the agency, not the production company — and often cutting while the shoot is still running. Post vendors are contracted in parallel with the production vendor, not downstream of it.
- PREP APPROVALS COLLAPSE INTO ONE MEETING. The PPM replaces weeks of scattered approvals: every location, cast option, wardrobe board, prop, product shot, timing and music direction is signed off in a single room against a bound book, days before the shoot.
- APPROVAL MOVES ONTO THE SHOOTING FLOOR. The sign-off a feature gets in dailies a week later happens here at the monitor, take by take, with the client in the room and a remote CMO on a stream. Approval is a live, time-consuming, clock-eating production activity.
- PICTURE LOCK IS THE BEGINNING OF THE WORK, NOT THE END. The master is the parent of a version tree: cutdowns (60/30/20/15/10/6), ratios (16:9, 9:16, 1:1, 4:5), languages, market-specific claims, retailer tags, endframes. Most of the finishing labour happens after 'final'.
- THE JOB'S PROFIT IS DECIDED 30-90 DAYS AFTER DELIVERY, at actualization, when a cost consultant reconciles the bid against the actual ledger. A delivered, awarded, beloved commercial can become a loss-making job months later.
- TALENT AND MUSIC RIGHTS KEEP RUNNING AFTER EVERYONE HAS GONE HOME. Holding fees, use fees and expiry dates continue on a 13-week clock for up to 21 months; the campaign has an ongoing cost curve that a film never has.

### How the crew differs

- PRESENT HERE, ABSENT FROM A FILM SET — THE CLIENT SIDE: Brand Manager, Marketing Director, CMO, client Regulatory/Legal counsel, and a Product or Brand Technician whose entire job is guarding the hero product and confirming the label is right. These people stand behind the monitor and approve takes in real time.
- PRESENT HERE — THE AGENCY SIDE: Executive Creative Director, Creative Director, and the Copywriter/Art Director team who wrote the board and defend it frame by frame; Account Director and Account Supervisor; Agency Producer / Executive Integrated Producer (the client's producer, who runs the client village, the approval ladder and post); Agency Business Affairs Manager (rights, usage, clearance); Head of Integrated Production (issues the bid list).
- PRESENT HERE — THE SELLING SIDE: the production company's Executive Producer, who is an owner and a salesperson rather than a film EP; the director's Sales Rep, a third party who represents directors to agencies for a commission; the Bid Producer, who builds losing budgets for a living.
- PRESENT HERE — THE MONEY POLICE: an independent production cost consultant retained by the agency or client to level bids and, months later, to disallow lines at actualization. No feature has an outside auditor sitting between the producer and the budget on every job.
- PRESENT HERE — THE PRODUCT SPECIALISTS: Food Stylist / Home Economist and assistants; Product Stylist and Model Maker; Tabletop Director and Tabletop DP; motion-control operator; high-speed (Phantom) technician; hand models, and a manicurist for them. An entire craft discipline exists to make a bottle, a burger or a pour behave on camera, and it barely exists in narrative film.
- PRESENT HERE: Wardrobe Stylist rather than Costume Designer (sourcing and options on boards, not design and make); groomer and beauty-specialist HMU for skin that will be seen at 4K macro; a brand guardian who sits in the grade to protect a Pantone value.
- PRESENT HERE — THE APPROVAL INFRASTRUCTURE: Video Assist / QTAKE operator running a client monitor and a secure remote stream (Sohonet ClearView, Evercast) so an absent CMO can veto live.
- PRESENT HERE — POST AS SEPARATE VENDORS: the Editor works at an independent editorial company hired directly by the agency, with the edit company's own Executive Producer and Post Producer; the finishing house supplies Flame artists, an Online Editor and a Colourist; a separate audio house supplies Sound Designer, Mix Engineer and VO direction. None of them report to the production company.
- PRESENT HERE — RIGHTS AND DELIVERY ADMIN: a talent payment house handling sessions, holding fees and use fees; a Broadcast Traffic Manager; an Ad-ID/clock-number administrator; a QC operator checking loudness and tech spec per broadcaster; and a clearance body examiner (Clearcast, or a network clearance department) who can reject the finished film.
- ABSENT: the Screenwriter, and with them the script coordinator, writers' assistant and the entire draft process. Also frequently absent: the Script Supervisor — a smaller continuity role may cover it, and on a tabletop day nobody is counting pages because there are none.
- ABSENT: the Unit Production Manager (a Production Manager and Line Producer do it), the 2nd 2nd AD and the deep AD tier, the studio's Production Executive, the completion bond representative, the Post Supervisor (the agency producer supervises post), and the Unit Publicist.
- ABSENT FROM POST: Supervising Sound Editor, Dialogue Editor, ADR Mixer, Foley Artist and Foley Mixer, Music Editor, Composer's orchestrator and a scoring stage. One sound designer and one mixer, one room, one or two days.
- ABSENT: Second Unit Director in the feature sense, the long-lead standing art department, dialect and acting coaches, and any producer acting as an author rather than a vendor's project manager. Nobody on this job owns the film.

### What goes wrong

- YOU LOSE THE BID. Three companies work, one is paid. Treatment, bid week, scout and rep time are gone, and the reason may be price, availability or a relationship you cannot see. Nothing else in film asks you to fully produce a pitch for free, repeatedly, as the normal cost of doing business.
- THE JOB IS KILLED AFTER AWARD. The client's budget is pulled, a competitor launches first, or the CMO changes. A cancellation schedule keyed to days-before-shoot pays a fraction (est.), crew must be released or paid, and the prep is worthless.
- THE BOARD CHANGES AFTER THE BID. Scope creep against a firm price: an extra location, a second cast member, a new product SKU. Every change is a change order you have to bill for and damage the relationship, or absorb out of the markup you were going to live on.
- THE PRODUCT REFUSES TO PERFORM. The beer pours with no head, the cheese will not stretch, the chocolate blooms under the lights, the ice cream is liquid in ninety seconds, the car paint reads brown, the liquid in the glass goes flat between the light and the take. A commercial can fail entirely because a physical substance did not behave, with the entire crew watching.
- THE HERO PRODUCT IS NOT THERE OR IS WRONG. Pre-launch packs with a label revision that has changed since approval, six units in existence and all of them tracked, a print run in the wrong CMYK, customs holding the shipment. You cannot shoot the advertisement for a product you do not have, and no amount of money fixes a six-week lead time inside a two-week prep.
- THE CLAIM CANNOT BE SUBSTANTIATED. The demo must be reproducible exactly as filmed; the evidence does not support the superlative; the comparative claim invites a rival's challenge. Legal changes the super, the super needs dwell time, the dwell time comes out of a fixed 30 seconds, and the picture has to change to make room for a sentence.
- CLEARANCE REJECTS THE FINISHED FILM. Clearcast or a network clearance department refuses it after online, grade and mix are paid for. You recut against a media buy that is already bought, and you may miss air.
- A REGULATOR OR COMPETITOR ATTACKS IT AFTER IT AIRS. An ASA ruling in the UK, an NAD challenge in the US, a rival's lawyers. The campaign is pulled mid-flight; the media is spent, the production is spent, and the brand wears the headline.
- THE FACE GOES OFF-BRAND. Your talent does something newsworthy after delivery. Morals clause invoked, campaign pulled, reshoot commissioned, every asset in every market recalled. A film survives a scandalous actor; an advertisement cannot.
- RIGHTS EXPIRE WHILE THE CAMPAIGN IS STILL WORKING. Holding fees lapse, the 21-month maximum period of use runs out, or the music licence was one year and the campaign is a hit. The performer or the publisher now holds all the leverage and prices accordingly. Success itself is the trigger for the cost.
- SOMETHING UNCLEARED IS IN FRAME. A competitor's logo, a piece of artwork, a building, a tattoo, a mural, a passer-by with no release, a magazine cover on a table. Every one is a paint-out, and at 4K on a 30-second spot that is seen thousands of times, somebody will notice.
- THE APPROVAL CHAIN DEADLOCKS OR REVERSES. The brand manager approves, their director reverses it, the CMO sees it for the first time at online and asks for a take that was never shot. Notes from different approvers contradict each other and nobody senior will choose. Rounds burn calendar you do not have.
- THE CLIENT LOSES THE AGENCY, OR THE AGENCY LOSES THE CLIENT, between shoot and air. Work in progress is orphaned; nobody remaining has the authority or the will to approve it.
- THE SPOT TESTS BADLY AFTER IT IS SHOT. A rough-cut score below norm can shelve a film that is already paid for, or force a recut that makes it worse — earlier logo, longer pack, harder VO — to move a number.
- THERE IS NO TOMORROW. One shoot day, one talent availability, one permit, one weather window. In a feature you pick it up next week; here the location is a working restaurant, the child has hit their legal hours, the celebrity flies at six, and the set is struck tonight because another production has the stage tomorrow. The day ends and whatever you did not get is a reshoot, a VFX bill, or a hole in the edit.
- THE CLOCK IS EATEN BY APPROVAL. Not by the work — by twelve people at a monitor asking for one more, a smile, a wider one, a version where the pack faces camera. The day ends in overtime and meal penalties incurred to satisfy people who do not know what an hour of it costs.
- THE VERSIONING MATRIX EXPLODES. A late change to the master re-renders and re-approves forty children: durations, ratios, languages, market claims, retailer tags. One German legal requirement or one retailer's endframe can multiply the finishing bill after the budget is closed.
- TECHNICAL DELIVERY FAILS AT THE GATE. Loudness out of spec, wrong clock number or Ad-ID, wrong slate, wrong frame rate for one broadcaster, QC bounce with no time to resubmit. The traffic deadline passes and the bought media runs something else — the single most expensive possible failure, because the media is the money.
- ACTUALIZATION TAKES THE PROFIT BACK. Ninety days after delivery a cost consultant disallows lines, queries the markup on a category, or refuses an overtime claim. The job you celebrated becomes the job that cost you.
- CASHFLOW KILLS A BUSY COMPANY. You paid crew, talent, stages and vendors on statutory timelines; the client pays net 90. Solvent on paper, insolvent in the bank, with three jobs in prep.
- THE DIRECTOR MAKES THEIR FILM INSTEAD OF THE CLIENT'S. Beautiful footage that does not cut to the approved board, no safeties, no pack shot that reads. The director always loses this argument, but the argument costs days, and sometimes the only usable frames are the ones nobody wanted to shoot.

### The shape of the money

FOUR SEPARATE POTS, AND THE BIGGEST ONE IS NOT PRODUCTION. The client pays: (1) an agency fee or retainer for creative and account service; (2) a production budget, approved as a pass-through against a bid; (3) rights — talent usage, music licensing, image and location rights, which run for years; and (4) MEDIA, which typically dwarfs all three. A common planning heuristic puts production at roughly 10% of campaign spend (est.; realistically 5-20%) — a $20m media buy behind a $1.5m production is unremarkable. Every production decision is therefore made in the shadow of a media commitment that is already paid for and cannot move. This is the deepest economic difference from every other kind of filmmaking: THE DEADLINE IS BOUGHT. INSIDE THE PRODUCTION BUDGET (the AICP bid, proportions are typical rather than fixed): crew labour across pre-production, shoot and wrap is usually the largest single block; then equipment, studio/stage, location, travel and the art department (set construction labour and materials can rival crew on a build job); then director and creative fees; then insurance (roughly 1-3% of direct costs, est.); then talent session fees and payroll handling; then a production fee — the company's markup on direct costs, commonly 20-35% (est.) — plus contingency and a weather day allowance. Editorial and finishing may sit inside the bid or, more often on larger jobs, be contracted separately by the agency. WHERE THE PROFIT ACTUALLY IS: not in the markup line, but in the difference between a FIRM BID and the actual ledger. On a firm bid the production company keeps the underage and absorbs the overage; on cost-plus, savings return to the client and the company earns only its fee. The entire commercial production business model is a bet, made weeks in advance, that the day will go as budgeted. Actualization 30-90 days later settles the bet — and a disallowed line or an unbudgeted overtime hour can flip a delivered, awarded job into a loss. BIDDING COSTS MONEY AND MOSTLY LOSES. Three companies produce a full creative treatment and a complete line-item budget; one gets paid. Treatment design, a bid producer's week and sometimes a scout are spent per bid, at a win rate commonly around one in three to one in five (est.). That sunk cost is embedded in the markup of the jobs you do win — the losers subsidise the winners. Nothing comparable exists in features. RIGHTS CAN EXCEED PRODUCTION. Session fees are small; usage is not. A US national campaign under the SAG-AFTRA Commercials Contract accrues holding fees every 13-week cycle plus per-use and market-based use fees across a maximum 21-month period of use, and heavy national rotation can pay a single principal more than the day rate of the whole crew. Celebrity deals are negotiated as flat buyouts outside the bid entirely, sometimes in the millions. Licensing a well-known music track for a national campaign commonly runs mid-five to seven figures (est.), split between master and publishing, and a custom composition is a fraction of that. WHY THE MONEY PER SHOOTING DAY IS SO DIFFERENT — THE DENOMINATOR, NOT THE NUMERATOR. A US national spot commonly costs $150,000-$500,000+ per shoot day all-in (est.); a mid-budget feature runs $50,000-$200,000 per day. The commercial day is not more expensive in kind — the crew rates are comparable, sometimes identical people. The difference is amortisation. Director, DP, casting, wardrobe, set build, location, insurance, prep, scouting, PPM, permits and the whole fixed apparatus are paid for by ONE TO THREE shooting days instead of forty. Prep is a fixed cost spread over a tiny base. Add compression premiums — everything booked at short notice, everything needed on a single date with no pickup day — and premium crew and kit hired at commercial rather than feature rates. THE PER-SECOND MATH TELLS THE TRUTH. A $500,000 two-day shoot delivering a 30-second spot is roughly $16,600 per finished second (production only, before media, rights and post). A $100m feature at 120 minutes is roughly $13,900 per finished second. A big commercial costs as much per second of screen time as a tentpole — and a $2m production for a 60-second film exceeds it outright. What a commercial actually buys is not a film, it is a guaranteed-quality 30 seconds that must survive being seen ten thousand times.

### Processes that exist only here

#### Client Brief Intake

Take and interrogate the client brief: objective, audience, proposition, mandatories, media plan and flight dates, budget band, regulatory constraints.

- **Who.** Account Director (agency) with the client's Brand/Marketing Director
- **Decides.** Accept, challenge or rewrite the brief. Whether to commit to the air date offered. The media flight you agree to becomes the immovable end of every later critical path.
- **Engine.** Brief object {objective, audience, proposition, mandatories[], media plan (channels, flight dates, spend), production band, legal constraints}. Air date drives a backward-scheduled critical path; every later task carries slack against it and slack can go negative.
- **Takes.** 1-5 days

#### Creative Brief and Territories

Convert the client brief into a single-minded creative brief and generate 3-8 strategic territories for creative teams to work against.

- **Who.** Creative Director / Strategy Director (agency)
- **Decides.** How tightly to brief, how many territories, how much risk to carry, whether to answer the brief or reframe it into a better question.
- **Engine.** Territory objects scored on {on-strategy, distinctiveness, producibility cost, legal risk, media fit}. Distinctiveness raises both award probability and client-rejection probability — the same axis, two opposite consequences.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days

#### Board and Script Development

Write the spot and board it: 12-30 numbered frames with shot description, cast, location type, product action, VO/dialogue and running seconds.

- **Who.** Copywriter and Art Director working as a creative team
- **Decides.** Format (film, tabletop, celebrity, VO-led, UGC-style), primary duration and which cutdowns are designed in, frame count, how producible it is at the budget band.
- **Engine.** Board = ordered frames {description, location type, cast, product action, VFX flag, seconds}. This is the only script object in the game; frame count and complexity feed both the bid estimator and the shoot-day setup model.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks

#### Internal Review / Tissue Session

Kill and sharpen work internally, then optionally share half-formed work with the client early (a tissue session) to de-risk the formal presentation.

- **Who.** Executive Creative Director with Account Director
- **Decides.** Which three routes go to the client and in what order; whether to show a safe route alongside a brave one, knowing the safe one usually wins.
- **Engine.** Presentation order anchors the client model. Client has memory of prior campaigns, an internal politics state, and a standing preference; showing a safe option materially raises the chance the brave one dies.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days

#### Claim Substantiation and Regulatory Pre-check

Test every claim, comparison, demo and super against evidence and codes before production money is committed. Demos must be reproducible exactly as depicted.

- **Who.** Agency Business Affairs / client Regulatory Affairs counsel
- **Decides.** Which claims survive, which soften to puffery, what evidence to commission, whether a comparative claim is worth the litigation exposure.
- **Engine.** Claim objects {type: absolute/comparative/puffery, required evidence, per-jurisdiction legality}. Failures emit mandatory supers with minimum dwell seconds and point size, which consume screen time inside a fixed duration.
- **Takes.** 3 days to 6 weeks if evidence must be generated

#### Client Presentation and Concept Approval

Sell the work into the room that pays for it, absorb notes, and leave with one route approved to produce.

- **Who.** Account Director presenting to Brand Manager, Marketing Director and sometimes CMO
- **Decides.** Which route to lead with, how hard to defend a detail, what to concede in the room to keep the idea alive.
- **Engine.** Approval gate with named approvers, each with authority level, agenda and risk appetite. A concession log binds later stages: anything conceded here becomes a mandatory the shoot and the edit must honour.
- **Takes.** 1 meeting; 3-15 days to written approval

#### Pre-testing (Copy Testing)

Test the animatic with consumers against category norms for attention, branding and emotional response before the production budget is released.

- **Who.** Consumer Insights Manager (client) with a research vendor (e.g. System1, Kantar, Ipsos)
- **Decides.** Whether to test at all, at what stage, against which norm set, and whether to obey a bad score or overrule it.
- **Engine.** Test model scoring branding moments, product screen time and distinctive assets, with noise and a documented bias toward convention. Score gates budget release and can be re-run at rough cut — where it can kill an already-shot film.
- **Takes.** 5-10 working days

#### Animatic / Ripomatic Build

Animate the board with scratch voice, temp music and borrowed footage so the client and the research can see something moving.

- **Who.** Agency Art Director with an animatic house and a scratch VO artist
- **Decides.** How much to spend making the idea look finished, and whether to use a temp track you cannot afford to license.
- **Engine.** Animatic quality raises both test score and client expectation, and expectation becomes the benchmark the finished film is judged against. Temp-track love is a tracked state that makes the real music decision harder and dearer.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days

#### Production Budget Setting and the Media Split

Fix the production budget band as a fraction of campaign spend, decide roster/preferred-vendor rules, decide whether production is decoupled from the agency.

- **Who.** Marketing Procurement Manager (client) with the agency's Head of Integrated Production
- **Decides.** Production budget, savings target, roster constraint, firm bid or cost-plus, whether the agency handles production money at all.
- **Engine.** Total campaign splits into agency fee / production / talent+music rights / media, with media typically the dominant number (production commonly cited around 10% of campaign spend — heuristic, varies 5-20%). Decoupling removes markup but adds coordination-failure probability.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks

#### Bid List and the Triple Bid

Issue the board, spec sheet, schedule assumptions and a blank AICP bid form to three production companies so the bids are comparable.

- **Who.** Agency Executive Producer (Head of Integrated Production)
- **Decides.** Which three companies; whether one is a courtesy bid to a favourite; what is specified versus left to interpretation; firm bid or cost-plus.
- **Engine.** Company records {director roster, reel fit, price index, reliability, availability}. Spec sheet fixes shoot days, crew size, cast count, locations, post scope. Identical specs are what make levelling possible — vagueness here creates unresolvable bid differences later.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days

#### Director Selection and Reel Review

Watch reels and choose which director on each company's roster to invite to pitch; check availability through the company's sales rep.

- **Who.** Agency Creative Director with the Agency Producer
- **Decides.** Which directors get the board; whether to chase an unavailable A-lister and slip the schedule to get them.
- **Engine.** Directors carry {genre strengths (comedy, performance, tabletop, beauty, car, doc-real), day rate, availability window, reel credibility, difficulty}. Director choice shifts execution-quality distributions on the day, not just cost.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days

#### Treatment Writing

Produce a 10-40 page interpretation: tone, references, casting approach, world, lighting, wardrobe, music, shot-by-shot intent, designed and typeset to sell.

- **Who.** Director, with the production company's Executive Producer and often a treatment writer/designer
- **Decides.** How far to deviate from the board, what to promise, what to leave vague, how much of the company's own money to spend on the document.
- **Engine.** Treatment {board fidelity, distinctiveness, deliverability risk}. Promises made here become obligations measured at PPM and on the day — and can be undeliverable at the bid price, which is a real and common trap.
- **Takes.** 3-7 days

#### The Pitch Call

Live call to the agency creatives and producer: walk the treatment, answer casting and feasibility questions, take the temperature of the client's fears.

- **Who.** Director, production company Executive Producer and the director's Sales Rep
- **Decides.** What to promise about cast, location and coverage; whether to push back on a frame that cannot be shot; whether to reveal the better idea now or after award.
- **Engine.** Pitch as a short move sequence (defend / concede / propose alternative) against modelled agency personalities. Outcome modifies win probability and writes verbal promises into the PPM agenda as checkable items.
- **Takes.** 30-60 minutes

#### Bid Build on the AICP Form

Build the estimate line by line on the industry standard form: pre-pro and wrap crew, shooting crew, location and travel, props/wardrobe/animals, studio and set construction labour and materials, equipment, media and data, miscellaneous, director/creative fees, insurance, talent, editorial — then the production fee.

- **Who.** Line Producer / Bid Producer (production company)
- **Decides.** Crew size, prep/shoot/wrap days, kit list, contingency and weather day, and the markup percentage (commonly 20-35% of direct costs — est.).
- **Engine.** Full line-item cost model with territory and union rate cards, 10/12-hour day rules, OT bands, meal penalties, kit rentals and prep days scaled to build size. Firm-bid vs cost-plus flag determines who keeps the underage — the single most important economic switch in the game.
- **Takes.** 3-7 days

#### Bid Levelling and the Cost Consultant

Lay three bids side by side, normalise differing assumptions, challenge rates and headcounts, and produce a levelled comparison for the client.

- **Who.** Agency Producer with an independent production cost consultant
- **Decides.** Where to cut, whose assumptions to impose on everyone, whether to re-bid, and which company to award once price and creative are weighed together.
- **Engine.** Normalising comparator across bids. Crucially, cuts propagate into the physics later: fewer crew slows setup times, fewer trucks lengthens turnaround, a dropped prep day raises the chance the set is not ready. Value engineering must have consequences.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days

#### Award, Kill Fees and the Two Lost Bids

Award the job at a negotiated number; the losing companies absorb their costs; cancellation terms attach to the winner.

- **Who.** Executive Producer (production company) and Agency Producer
- **Decides.** Whether to accept the awarded figure after cuts or walk; how much sunk cost to keep chasing; whether to take a loss-leader for the relationship.
- **Engine.** Sunk-cost ledger per bid (treatment, bid producer days, scout, rep time). Win rate around one in three to one in five (est.). Cancellation schedule keyed to days-before-shoot, rising toward full bid inside 48 hours (est.).
- **Takes.** 1-3 days

#### Purchase Order, Terms and Cashflow

Agree the payment schedule and contract terms, raise the PO, and fund a shoot that must be paid for before the client pays.

- **Who.** Head of Production (production company) with client Procurement and Accounts Payable
- **Decides.** Staged payments (commonly 50/40/10) versus payment on delivery; whether to accept net-60/90 terms; whether to take the job at all on those terms.
- **Engine.** Company working-capital model. Crew payroll, talent sessions and vendor invoices have legally fixed timing that client payment terms do not respect; a solvent company with a full order book can fail on the float.
- **Takes.** 1-2 weeks to issue; net 30-90 to pay

#### Crewing Up

Book the shooting crew for specific dates at short notice: DP, gaffer, key grip, 1st AD, camera, art, sound, and a full prep and wrap complement.

- **Who.** Production Manager / Line Producer
- **Decides.** Which DP (the director's regular or the one who is free), local versus travelling crew, day rate versus negotiated deal, how deep to crew the art department.
- **Engine.** Crew pool with availability calendars, rates, speed and quality modifiers, and loyalty to specific directors. Commercial crew work job-to-job, so at 10 days' notice the good ones are already booked — availability is a genuine, biting constraint.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days

#### Casting Brief and Casting Session

Brief to a look and a feeling rather than a character; see 20-80 people per role; tape improvised moments, product interactions, and 'real people' street casting where the brief demands authenticity.

- **Who.** Casting Director (commercials specialist)
- **Decides.** Union or non-union, actor versus real person versus model, how many to see, what to make them do on tape, whether to cast hands separately.
- **Engine.** Talent pool {look tags, skills (comedy, dialogue, physical, hand model), union status, quote, existing product-category conflicts}. Conflict checking is a hard rule: a performer held by a competitor cannot be booked at any price.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days of sessions

#### Callbacks and Client Casting Approval

Shortlist to a selects tape, run callbacks, and put the final choice in front of the client, who chooses the face of the campaign.

- **Who.** Agency Creative Director and the client's Brand Manager
- **Decides.** Whether to fight for the better performer or take the client's pick; how many options to show; whether to hide the option you do not want chosen.
- **Engine.** Client preference model distinct from the performance model — the client's favourite can genuinely be the weaker performer, and that must be allowed to happen and to cost the player on the day and in the edit.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days

#### Talent Deal, Usage and Buyout

Negotiate session fee plus rights: under the SAG-AFTRA Commercials Contract, holding fees per 13-week cycle, Class A/network use, wild spot by market, cable and digital use, within a maximum 21-month period of use; or a negotiated flat buyout for non-union and international work.

- **Who.** Agency Business Affairs Manager with talent agents
- **Decides.** Term, media, territory and exclusivity; 13-week cycles versus 1-year versus 3-year worldwide; union or non-union; principal versus extra (one line of dialogue upgrades an extra to a principal).
- **Engine.** Live per-performer usage ledger {term, media, territory, cycle dates, renewal price, expiry} that keeps running long after delivery. On-set events reclassify performers (a face comes into focus, an extra speaks) and retro-bill the production.
- **Takes.** 1-5 days; weeks for a celebrity

#### Music Strategy and Rights Clearance

Search, brief demos, choose between a licensed track, custom composition, a re-record/sound-alike, or library — and clear it for the same term, media and territory as the campaign.

- **Who.** Music Supervisor or a music house producer, with Agency Business Affairs
- **Decides.** Famous track versus commissioned versus library; whether to cut to the track; how to handle a client who loves the unaffordable temp.
- **Engine.** Master and publishing rights held separately; any one co-writer can refuse. Price scales with fame x term x media x territory. An edit locked to a track makes a late refusal catastrophic — the failure must be able to land after picture lock.
- **Takes.** 1-6 weeks

#### Location Scout, Recce and Permits

Find and secure locations that carry the board, a client audience and a lighting package; then a technical recce with department heads; then permits, neighbours and police.

- **Who.** Location Manager with the Director and DP
- **Decides.** Real location versus studio build versus stage-and-extend; how many locations one day can carry; whether the client can be comfortably parked there.
- **Engine.** Locations {travel time, permit lead, power, sun path, noise, neighbour risk, client-village suitability}. A company move costs 60-120 minutes of a 10-hour day. Client comfort is a real variable because the client is physically present.
- **Takes.** 3-10 days; permits 3-15 business days

#### Studio, Set Build and Cyc

Design and build sets, cycloramas and product tables inside a prep window measured in days, with the client approving drawings and finishes.

- **Who.** Production Designer with a Construction Coordinator
- **Decides.** Build versus found; number of build days; which walls are hero and which are cheated; paint finish and sheen under the lighting package.
- **Engine.** Build days consume stage rental and labour before a frame is shot. A set one day late loses the shoot day outright, because talent, crew and client are booked for that single date and cannot simply be pushed.
- **Takes.** 2-10 days

#### Wardrobe Sourcing, Fittings and Board Approval

Source 3-5 options per character, shoot fitting photos, present them as boards at the PPM, and carry approved alternates to set.

- **Who.** Wardrobe Stylist with a costume assistant
- **Decides.** Which looks to present and which to hide; what reads on camera next to the pack colours; how many backups per performer.
- **Engine.** Costume options with colour values that interact with brand colour rules and the grade. Client rejection on the day is a live event the truck's alternates must answer, or the setup stalls.
- **Takes.** 3-7 days

#### Hero Product Supply and Chain of Custody

Get the advertised product to set in the correct batch and label revision, in controlled quantities, often pre-launch, with every unit logged, guarded and returned or destroyed.

- **Who.** Prop Master with the client's Product/Brand Technician
- **Decides.** How many hero units to order and how early; mock-ups versus real; who is allowed to touch them; what happens to units consumed on camera.
- **Engine.** Product inventory {unit count, lead time, label version, consumption per take, condition}. Running out of hero units ends the shoot regardless of daylight, crew or money remaining. Lead times can exceed the entire prep window.
- **Takes.** 1-6 weeks lead time

#### Pack Preparation and Label Work

Prepare packs for camera: de-glare, clean, hand-apply corrected labels, build oversized or cutaway comps, ensure the label sits square and the brand colour reads true.

- **Who.** Product Stylist / Model Maker
- **Decides.** Real pack versus built pack versus CG pack; how many prepped units per setup; whether to fix in post or on the table.
- **Engine.** Each prepped unit has a hero-condition value degrading per take (fingerprints, condensation, dust, scuffs). Resets cost minutes. Usable takes before reset is a hard per-setup budget that the director's appetite for takes runs into.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days prep plus continuous on-set work

#### Food Styling and Home Economics

Make food behave and look right under hot lights: sear, pour, melt, stretch, steam, glisten, hold — cooking dozens of identical batches for a single ten-second shot.

- **Who.** Food Stylist / Home Economist with assistants
- **Decides.** How many batches to prepare, what may be substituted, when to plate relative to the camera being ready, whether the shot is one chance or repeatable.
- **Engine.** Per-dish camera-life timer from plate to death; batch count; timed windows for steam, pour, melt. A regulatory flag separates the advertised product (must be the genuine article, honestly represented) from set dressing (may be faked) — different rules, same frame.
- **Takes.** 1 prep day plus the shoot day

#### Tabletop / Product Unit

Shoot the product action — pours, splashes, drips, hero rotations, macro texture, ingredient hits — usually on a separate stage or day from the live-action unit, with motion control and high-speed cameras.

- **Who.** Tabletop Director with a specialist Tabletop DP, motion control operator and high-speed technician
- **Decides.** Motion control versus handheld macro; frame rate; number of passes; practical versus CG; whether the beauty shot is bought on the day or built later.
- **Engine.** Motion-control rigs with programmable repeatable passes that composite in post; frame rate multiplies light requirement and shrinks the real-world event window (a 1000fps pour is roughly 40 milliseconds of reality per second of screen time).
- **Takes.** 1-3 days

#### Children, Babies and Animals

Work minors and animals inside legal and welfare limits that cannot be bent, in a day that has no tomorrow.

- **Who.** Studio Teacher / Chaperone and Animal Wrangler
- **Decides.** Real animal or CG; how many twins or identical animals to book; where in the day to place their scenes; how much of the board depends on an uncooperative performer.
- **Engine.** Hard per-jurisdiction limits on minors' on-set hours by age, with breaks and schooling; animal performance as stochastic with a fatigue curve; doubles reset the clock. These constraints reorder the day because they are inviolable.
- **Takes.** 1 week prep; restricted hours on the day

#### Shooting Board and Day Plan

Turn the approved board into an ordered setup list with time estimates: light, rehearse, turn over, takes, reset — and a plan for what is sacrificed if the day slips.

- **Who.** 1st Assistant Director with the Director
- **Decides.** Setup order, which boarded frames are protected, where the product beauty sits, what gets dropped first, when to call the cut and move.
- **Engine.** Setups as atomic units {light minutes, rehearse, take duration, reset, risk}. A running day clock with meal penalties, 10/12-hour marks, OT bands and next-day turnaround. Progress is setups completed against the board, never pages.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days to build; executed over the shoot

#### The Client Village and Live Approval

House the agency and client at a monitor beside the set and take their approval take by take, in real time, while the crew stands by.

- **Who.** Agency Producer running the client monitor; Creative Director seated beside the Brand Manager
- **Decides.** What to show them and when; whether to give them the extra take; how to spend the political capital of saying no in front of the client's boss.
- **Engine.** Per-take approval state from named approvers, an options queue (a safety, a smile, a wider one, one where the pack faces camera), and each request costing clock. Approver satisfaction is a spendable, depletable resource.
- **Takes.** The entire shooting day

#### Remote Approval Stream

Stream the monitor feed to absent decision-makers — a CMO in another city, a global brand team in another timezone — with colour and latency caveats.

- **Who.** Video Assist / QTAKE Operator with a secure streaming service (e.g. Sohonet ClearView, Evercast)
- **Decides.** Who gets a feed and at what quality; whether an off-site approver holds a live veto; whether to stream at all.
- **Engine.** Additional approver nodes with lag and reduced context — they see the frame, not the room, not the light, not the performer's third take. A remote note can arrive after the setup is struck, creating a forced choice between rebuilding and refusing.
- **Takes.** Continuous during the shoot

#### Coverage, Safeties and the Version Options

Decide when a shot is finished, and whether to buy in camera the alternatives the versioning matrix will demand later: alternate endframes, product swaps, shorter beats, vertical framing.

- **Who.** Director with the 1st AD and the Agency Producer
- **Decides.** Call it or go again; give the agency the option; shoot the vertical safe frame; shoot the alternate ending for the 15-second cutdown.
- **Engine.** Per-take quality drawn with performance and technical components. Options bought in camera make the version tree cheap; options skipped become post costs or reshoots. An explicit, visible trade of day clock against post budget.
- **Takes.** Minutes to hours per setup

#### Stills and Social Capture in Parallel

Capture the print, OOH, packaging and social assets on the same set, the same day, the same talent and product — under a separate rights grant.

- **Who.** Stills Photographer with a Content/Social Producer
- **Decides.** Stolen minutes between setups versus a dedicated day; what the integrated campaign actually needs; whether to grant stills usage now or pay to re-shoot later.
- **Engine.** A parallel asset requirement list competing for the same setups, talent minutes and product units. Stills usage is a separate term/media/territory deal with its own price. Skipping it means re-shooting half the campaign at full cost.
- **Takes.** Stolen hours, or 1 dedicated day

#### Wrap, Data and the Actual Ledger

Strike, return kit, account for hero product, back up and verify media, and start the actual-cost ledger against the bid while everyone's timecards are fresh.

- **Who.** Production Manager with the DIT and Data Wrangler
- **Decides.** What goes to editorial tonight, what redundancy the media gets, which overages to flag now versus argue later.
- **Engine.** Media chain of custody with verified redundant copies. Overtime, meal penalties, damage and overages accrue into the actual ledger from the instant they occur, running silently against the firm bid's margin.
- **Takes.** 2-6 hours after wrap; wrap days follow

#### Selects and First Assembly

Pull selects, build the board as shot, and produce a first cut — frequently against a temp or the licensed track, with cutdowns roughed at the same time.

- **Who.** Editor at an independent editorial company, with an Assistant Editor
- **Decides.** Which takes; whether to cut the board or cut the better film that was actually shot; whether to show the deviation at all.
- **Engine.** Edit state over takes with a board-fidelity score alongside a quality score. A cut that is better but unfaithful can be rejected precisely because it is not what was sold to the client — the two scores must be able to diverge.
- **Takes.** 2-5 days

#### Offline Rounds and the Approval Ladder

Carry the cut up a ladder of approvers, each of whom has notes and none of whom has seen the shoot.

- **Who.** Agency Producer, routing cuts to Creative Director, then Account, then Brand Manager, then Marketing Director, then CMO
- **Decides.** How many rounds to budget; what to show whom first; whether to present one cut or two; which contradictory notes to obey.
- **Engine.** Approval DAG with per-node latency, agenda and veto power. Notes from different nodes can be mutually contradictory, and the engine must never silently reconcile them — resolving the contradiction is the player's job and costs rounds.
- **Takes.** 1-3 weeks, 3-8 rounds

#### Rough-cut Testing

Put the real cut into the same norm-based test used on the animatic, after the money is spent.

- **Who.** Consumer Insights Manager with the research vendor
- **Decides.** Whether to test; whether to obey a below-norm score with a recut, a reshoot, or by shelving the film.
- **Engine.** Score driven by branding moments, product screen seconds, distinctive asset presence and a decay curve on attention. The available fixes (earlier logo, longer pack shot, harder VO) reliably raise the score and reliably damage the film.
- **Takes.** 5-10 working days

#### VFX, Beauty and Clean-up

Paint out logos and rigs, replace screens, fix skin and hair, composite motion-control passes, swap or build packs in CG, fix what the client changed their mind about.

- **Who.** VFX Supervisor and Flame/Nuke Artists at a finishing house
- **Decides.** What is fixed versus built; whether to make a CG pack asset so future market versions are cheap; how much retouching is legally and ethically permissible.
- **Engine.** Per-shot VFX task list with hours and dependencies. A reusable CG pack asset lowers the marginal cost of every later version. Some jurisdictions restrict or require disclosure of digitally altered body imagery — a rule, not a preference.
- **Takes.** 3 days to 6 weeks

#### Online Conform, Grade and Brand Colour Approval

Conform at full resolution, grade the film, and prove the pack and logo colours still match the brand reference under the look.

- **Who.** Colourist and Online Editor, with a brand guardian from the client in the room
- **Decides.** The look, and whether to sacrifice it where it moves the brand red; how many grade rounds to carry; whether to grade the pack separately with a window.
- **Engine.** Brand colour spec as reference values per pack element, checked against graded frames. A grade that flatters the film and shifts the red gets rejected. Each round is suite time at a day rate against a finite finishing budget.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days

#### Sound Design, VO Record and Mix

Build effects, record and direct voiceover, sit the music under the claim, and mix to broadcast loudness spec.

- **Who.** Sound Designer and Mix Engineer, with the VO Artist directed live by the Creative Director
- **Decides.** VO casting and read direction; how many alternate reads to keep for versions; where the legal super lands against the copy; music level under dialogue.
- **Engine.** Mix over stems against a hard loudness target (US CALM Act / ATSC A/85 measured per ITU-R BS.1770 at -24 LKFS; EBU R128 at -23 LUFS in Europe) that is pass/fail at QC. VO talent carries its own session-and-usage deal with its own expiry.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days

#### Clearance and Broadcast Approval

Submit script at concept stage and the finished film at the end, with substantiation attached, and obtain approval and an identifier — or a rejection with conditions.

- **Who.** Agency Business Affairs with the clearance body (Clearcast in the UK; network clearance departments in the US)
- **Decides.** What to submit and when; how to argue a marginal claim; whether to change the film or change the claim; which territories to clear separately.
- **Engine.** Per-territory clearance object with rule set, review latency, and outcomes {approved, approved with changes, rejected}. Rejection after online forces a recut against a media buy that is already paid for and already booked.
- **Takes.** 2-10 working days per submission

#### Versioning Matrix

Build every deliverable from the approved master: durations (60/30/20/15/10/6), aspect ratios (16:9, 9:16, 1:1, 4:5), languages, market-specific claims and supers, endframes, retailer tags, platform-specific social cuts.

- **Who.** Post Producer at the editorial/finishing company
- **Decides.** What is cut down from the master versus designed natively; which markets get bespoke claims; how many versions the budget can actually carry.
- **Engine.** Version tree where each node inherits from a parent and carries deltas {duration, ratio, language, super text, endframe, SKU, music edit}. Changing the master re-renders and re-approves every child; each child needs its own clearance and its own QC.
- **Takes.** 2-10 days; longer for global rollouts

#### Supers, Endframes and Mandatories

Set the legal supers, brand lockup, endframe and any URL or price to brand guidelines and legibility rules, in every language and every ratio.

- **Who.** Motion Graphics Designer / Typographer
- **Decides.** Placement and dwell of legal copy; how much of a 30-second film the disclaimer is allowed to eat; whether the endframe holds long enough to register.
- **Engine.** Supers carry minimum dwell seconds and minimum size per territory and cannot overlap forbidden zones. They consume seconds from a fixed duration, so a legal change is a picture change and can force a re-edit.
- **Takes.** 1-2 days

#### Deliverables, Identifier and Trafficking

Register the identifier (Ad-ID in the US, a Clearcast-issued clock number in the UK), master to each broadcaster's tech spec, pass QC, and deliver before the traffic deadline.

- **Who.** Broadcast Traffic Manager with a distribution platform (e.g. Extreme Reach, Adstream, Peach)
- **Decides.** How much deadline buffer to hold; how many station-specific variants to cut; who signs the QC off.
- **Engine.** Hard traffic deadlines relative to air date, per-broadcaster spec sheets, QC bounce-and-resubmit latency. Missing the deadline means the bought media runs a competitor's slot or the old spot — the media money is the biggest money in the campaign.
- **Takes.** 1-3 days; deadline 2-5 business days before air

#### Actualization and Final Cost Report

Reconcile every AICP line against real spend, weeks after delivery, with receipts, timecards, invoices and disputed overages.

- **Who.** Line Producer with the agency's cost consultant and the client's auditor
- **Decides.** What to absorb, what to bill as an overage, what to fight; whether to concede a disallowed line to protect the relationship.
- **Engine.** Actual ledger versus bid ledger per line. Firm bid: the company keeps the underage and eats the overage — this is where the profit actually lives. Cost-plus: savings return to the client and the company earns only the fee. Disallowances can turn a delivered job into a loss.
- **Takes.** 30-90 days after wrap

#### Usage Administration and Renewal

Pay session fees, holding fees each 13-week cycle and use fees as the spot airs; track music, image and location rights expiry against the media plan.

- **Who.** Business Affairs Manager with a talent payment house (e.g. Extreme Reach/Talent Partners, Cast & Crew)
- **Decides.** Renew, let lapse, or reshoot; whether to keep paying to hold a performer off a competitor; whether a hit campaign is worth the renegotiated price.
- **Engine.** Running clock per performer, track and asset {cycle dates, renewal price, expiry, exclusivity}. Renewal price rises with the campaign's own success (the hold-up problem). An ad airing past expiry is an active liability, reconciled against the media plan.
- **Takes.** 13-week cycles across a 21-month maximum period of use

#### Effect Measurement, Awards and the Case Film

Measure brand lift, attention and sales effect; enter Cannes Lions, D&AD, Effies; cut a case film that retells the campaign as a success story.

- **Who.** Brand Manager with the agency Strategy Director and an awards entry producer
- **Decides.** What to claim and to whom; which awards to enter and at what entry cost; whether the case film is honest.
- **Engine.** Effect model linking media weight x creative quality x distinctive branding to outcome, with lag and noise. A separate jury model rewards distinctiveness. Both feed agency reputation, director reel value, and who gets invited onto the next bid list.
- **Takes.** 4 weeks to 12 months
